## AI Research

### [Wah wah wah wah do more AI R&D](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pY76hHbdD4jduX3YH/wah-wah-wah-wah-do-more-ai-r-and-d)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h1>Ice cream</h1>
<p>There's a particular kind of listening that kids (and adults) sometimes do. Daddy is saying stuff—his mouth is moving and making noise—and he's talking about what I ate for lunch, and something about other times I've eaten ice cream, and something about rules and this and that and what Mommy would think and something about bedtime—but I am not so interested in that. What interests me is the punchline "...and therefore you can't have ice cream.". I'm only incidentally interested in the other stuff, insofar as it might determine whether I get ice cream.</p>
<p>In an obvious way, I'm showing rather poor listening skills. I did not understand most of what Daddy said, and arguably I'm even being rude.</p>
<p>However, in another way, this stance has a virtue. The virtue is that it holds fast to what matters, as best I know how, and does not give in to Daddy's framing. It sticks to what is real and concrete, what matters, despite a cloud of justifications. This is certainly not an unalloyed virtue, just as the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_purpose_of_a_system_is_what_it_does">POSIWID</a> perspective is both useful and fraught. But it matters that although Daddy is a complex system with internal states, his internal complexity can, in one sense, be largely ignored and summarized simply as "an entity that prevents ice cream".</p>
<p>Sure, something something lunch and real food, but those are matters for your concern—what do they mean for me and ice cream? I want ice cream. You've made some sort of background assumptions about what implications "real food" should have, regarding the question of ice cream, and I am not on board with whatever those assumptions may be, even if I cannot immediately give you a detailed exposition of what they are and why I disagree.</p>
<h1>Wah wah</h1>
<p>In the TV cartoon of Charlie Brown, the adults talk, but only in trombone, like "wah wah wah wah": <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=oHdlS3XZLBo">https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=oHdlS3XZLBo</a></p>
<p>The kids can understand what the adults are saying, but the two groups mostly don't participate in each other's worlds. The kids interact enough to handle the functional situation with the adult, but they aren't engaging deeply with the adult's considerations except insofar as they bear on the kid's situation.</p>
<h1>Scammer</h1>
<p>Your bank tells you: Don't give out your passwords or account information on the phone. We won't ever ask you for that.</p>
<p>That's the advice. They don't say, "Evaluate whether the caller is legitimate, and whether the circumstances they describe are circumstances in which you ought to give them your bank account info.". That's not a good stance. That's hackable. Just don't give out your bank account info on the phone.</p>
<h1>Throwing stuff on a bonfire to make it burn less</h1>
<p>In "<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/yaz8nx4ogZmiqHzt7/what-just-happened-pragmatism-and-pessimization">What just happened? Pragmatism and Pessimization</a>", Ngo details a history of clever reasons that producing more AI capabilities helps decrease existential risk from AI capabilities. We have had, and continue to have, a variety of such clever reasons, very often coming from people who also admit several percentage points of risk of the technology killing everyone:</p>
<ul>
<li>OpenAI, xAI. These are your classic disaster monkeys; chaotic, freaked out, but charmingly (in a way) obvious about intentionally mutating programs in order to create a world-eating machine. There's a thin veneer of justification for how this helps safety, something about open-source or truth-seeking or something, and then a healthily-poorly-rationalized stance of "fuck-it".</li>
<li>Anthropic. This organization seems to be the heart, or locus of condensation, of (complicated, incoherent, indefensible) justifications for accelerating AI capabilities even though AI might eat the world<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-gjmyxwg8R3gS6gsHP-1">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-gjmyxwg8R3gS6gsHP-1">[1]</a></sup>
    </span>. Much ink could be spilled, but my childish listening just hears "wah wah wah wah do more AI R&amp;D".</li>
<li>Some people at Redwood are <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/tQHeEzKqK3awL2RxR/introducing-the-conceptual-reasoning-index">trying to make AIs better at abstract reasoning when verified rewards are scarce</a>. The idea is to improve AI's abstract reasoning so that AI is more useful for doing alignment research. We could argue about whether this makes sense, but in this essay, all I hear is "wah wah wah wah make the AI better at thinking difficult thoughts".</li>
<li>"<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pFzctpJBat95SrCyC/ai-2040-plan-a">AI 2040: Plan A</a>" proposes that we stop all capabilities research, and then we continue almost all of the capabilities research, but also publish all the algorithms we're using. The idea is to use increased capabilities to do more alignment research before the pause breaks down. I believe this makes no sense, and instead you should just ban all the research. E.g. because either the difference in capabilities didn't matter much, in which case why not just stop; or else it did matter, in which case you are making the situation way worse by increasing the capabilities lying around after the pause breaks down, by pushing capabilities right up close to the point of an intelligence explosion (which, trust us, we can totally tell when that's about to happen). But before arguing that, in this essay all I hear is "wah wah wah wah keep the hundreds of researchers working on making AI capabilities".</li>
</ul>
<p>To invert <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=huPtWcb982o">BoJack Horseman</a>, when you put your glasses back on, all the differential acceleration just looks like acceleration.</p>
<h1>A skip connection for sanity</h1>
<p><em>(H/t <a href="https://kaverennedy.substack.com/p/the-skip-connection-theory-of-everything">Kave for the productivization of the idea of skip connections</a>.)</em></p>
<p>People who are going "wah wah wah wah do more AI R&amp;D" might say things like:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>[we can accelerate some specific AI capabilities, and we should do that, so] Which AI capabilities should we accelerate?</p>
</blockquote>
<p>or</p>
<blockquote>
<p>[we can accelerate AI capabilities and keep them siloed enough that we get a lead and can invest in controlling those capabilities, and we should do that, so] How do we control the AI capabilities we're making?</p>
</blockquote>
<p>or</p>
<blockquote>
<p>[we can accelerate AI capabilities and keep them siloed enough that we get a lead and can invest in alignment research, and we should do that, so] How do we use our increased AI capabilities to do more alignment research?</p>
</blockquote>
<p>And then we get tangled up in considering all that stuff. I conjecture that many employees of Anthropic allowed themselves to be misled in this way. I conjecture that lots of people all throughout the AGI existential derisking space get misled in this way. I conjecture that you've been hacked out of the obvious view—AI capabilities are dangerous, so more is worse—by Dario, by Sam Altman, by money, by your project manager.</p>
<p>My hope is that those people will try on the "wah wah wah" way of listening, sometimes. If you consider yourself in favor of human flourishing, notice that it doesn't make sense to build the thing that would eat everything including humans; and it still doesn't make sense even with a bunch of arguments that may have big holes in them.</p>
<p>To put this another way, I think it's often virtuous to have <em>skip connections</em> in your epistemology. Yes, there's a bunch of details to think about, and the details matter, and they can lead to nonobvious conclusions, and other people may understand things better than you do. So there's a blob of thinking that's good for you to do, a big blob of compute as it were, to think through all those details and considerations.</p>
<p>But it's also good to have some skip connections that mostly go around that blob of thinking, and directly get a say in final considerations. At some point in the past or coming months, you may face some decisions about whether to leave your job, speak out about the existential risk of AI, call for a pause, push back on arguments for advancing capabilities, or advocate internally and pressure leadership to support a pause and shut down capabilities research. You may have a sense like the following:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>"AI is very dangerous. Maybe we shouldn't do AI. If someone says we should do AI, maybe they are wrong. (Maybe they are using motivated reasoning that's wrong, but confusing enough to confuse me if I already somewhat buy into their way of seeing the world, and not easy to refute in a short paragraph that can, in one stroke, overcome a network of rationalizations and distortions.)"</p>
</blockquote>
<p>That sense is good, whether or not other arguments should change your final decision. In your decision-making, your fundamental values should get a say, and your simpler, more childish understanding of what people are arguing about should also get a say.</p>
<p>You probably agree <em>in theory</em> that naive views of a situation should continue to get some sort of say in final judgements. However, <strong>implementing this theory often takes active work and doesn't happen by default</strong>. There's a set of forces like social pressure, <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/jzy5qqRuqA9iY7Jxu/the-problem-of-graceful-deference-1">(reasonable) deference</a>, skillful frame control from leaders, general <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive_miser">cognitive miserliness</a>, and <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/sinclair-s-razor">Sinclair's Razor</a>, which together make it natural to not track the hypothesis that you should stop increasing AI capabilities. Tracking a hypothesis takes work. In particular, it takes work to do these two things at the same time:</p>
<ol>
<li>Hear someone arguing for some conclusion that you partly want to believe.</li>
<li>And yet, continue to fully track that the conclusion might be false, and that your unsophisticated worry still makes sense to think about and stand firmly on, even if it's only based on the overall punchline of what they are saying.</li>
</ol>
<p>That work is worthwhile. Listening like a child might be a helpful tool in that work.</p>
<ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-gjmyxwg8R3gS6gsHP-1"><p>[The following will contain multiple strawmen of any one person's position. However, all of the following is based on things I have heard from many different people either at Anthropic or defending Anthropic's behavior, in a mix of some first-hand and mainly second-or-more-hand. What these stances have in common is that they all say "wah wah wah wah do more AI R&amp;D". With that in mind, my thread of childish listening hears Anthropic, as a collective, say the following:] We have to make AI because we want to slow down AI. But we can't slow down AI because OpenAI won't slow down. If they say they'll slow down, we don't trust them. Did we talk to them? Nah, we don't trust them, there's no point. And anyway, there's China. And if <em>they</em> say <em>they'll</em> slow down, we don't trust them either. Did we talk to them? Nah. Other people are reckless. We are not reckless. That is why we should advance AI capabilities. At the right time, we will stop. But not before. If we stop before the right time, then we might not stop enough. The politicians, they won't want to stop. Did we talk to them? No, there's no point, they don't understand; we have to make them understand by not stopping, and then they'll listen when we say to stop. Well, we'll talk to them because we need them to enforce export controls because China is bad and reckless, so that we can race to the top. This is different from why other people want a lead in AI. They want a lead because they are greedy and reckless. We want a lead because we are good and responsible. We would do AI safety and we would do nice things with an aligned AI, or maybe our AI should take over the world. The only way to stop is to not stop. That way later you can stop more. Should we say publicly that we want to stop? Eh. Sure, I guess. I mean, it's in some blog post somewhere. What the heck do you want from me? What, am I going to spend 10 hours a day nonstop talking about how I love stopping AI research? I got stuff to do. There's AI capabilities to be developed. Plus, if we say we should stop, Trump might not like us, and then we won't be able to continue our research. Our research is good because it will help get a stop eventually, if it's the case that we get strong evidence that we can't solve alignment. We have to publicly politically support AI capabilities so that later we can support a stop. Dario has a plan. He's good. He'll stop. Our leadership is <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/5aKRshJzhojqfbRyo/unless-its-governance-changes-anthropic-is-untrustworthy">trustworthy</a> and <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qxAroimgs4BvBHz2i/anthropic-does-not-understand-why-its-draft-bill-was">strategic</a>. Alignment might be pretty doable actually. So more AI capabilities are good actually. Did we argue that? Well, someone here read your blogposts. We believe we have understood your blogposts and taken them into account, thank you for your concern. Argue it? Why bother? We know what is going on, and you do not know what is going on, so how could you know that we won't solve alignment? We have the private information, and you do not. Sure, of course there's a 40% chance we'll kill everyone, but if China does it, then there will be a 70% chance. And if China would stop, well, there's open-source. You can't stop open-source. We have to develop our AIs that will get distilled into open-source models so that we can stop open-source models. Beff Jezos might take over the world, which would be bad, so we need hundreds of AI scientists to try to increase capabilities as fast as possible in order to stop him. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-gjmyxwg8R3gS6gsHP-1">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
</ol>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pY76hHbdD4jduX3YH/wah-wah-wah-wah-do-more-ai-r-and-d#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Greater good arguments in AI safety](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/WRvxRHMCmc3Qt4axz/greater-good-arguments-in-ai-safety)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Back in the before-time of the 2010s, when EA was still known mostly for funding health efforts in the developing world, there were many baskets of criticism floating around from the left. The most prominent was a cluster centered around EAs being white men, and therefore suspicious.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref7xwx74xqbo9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn7xwx74xqbo9">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Another category, in my experience the second-most common, was that in attempting to rectify the problems in the world, EA was masking, whitewashing, and misdirecting efforts away from solving the "real problem", usually capitalism but often other contradictory forces. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These arguments were often not specific to EA, but mainstream arguments against charitable giving in general. On any Hacker News post about a billionaire giving to charity, one of the first few comments would inevitably be this Oscar Wilde quote:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Just as the worst slave-owners were those who were kind to their slaves, and so prevented the horror of the system being realized by those who suffered from it, and understood by those who contemplated it, so, in the present state of things in England, the people who do most harm are the people who try to do most good.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefj4zxm1qgqdt"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnj4zxm1qgqdt">[2]</a></sup></span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These arguments have a long history. Anticapitalist critiques of charity go back to Engels and Marx, who called charitable givers a bourgeois faction intent on "redressing social grievances in order to secure the continued existence of bourgeois society." In their most explicit form these critiques had the following shape:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There is a "root cause" of most societal ills, such as capitalism. </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Billionaires giving to deworming efforts addresses the symptoms of this problem, such as poverty and disease. But it's not treating the root cause. </span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Additionally, a side effect of such giving is that it gives people reason to tolerate the current system better, or even begin to like their oppressors, which gets in the way of fomenting change.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So actually, deworming people in Africa, or giving legal aid to poor people in the U.S., is net-negative. You should just spend all of your time trying to change capitalism.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefox69qtkq9w"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnox69qtkq9w">[3]</a></sup></span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">While you could argue with these proposals on the object level, I think most people have an intuitive sense that this line of thinking is fraught, independent of whatever political program is mentioned. One general concern you might have is that if you're going to claim that the first-order effects of someone's good work are swamped by some second-order effect, particularly some second-order effect on </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">optics</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, then the burden of proof is pretty high. And clearly, the people making these arguments do not have a good enough model of political economy to clear that bar. Not only is it not obvious that billionaire charity makes people like billionaires more, it is also not clear that the detractor's preferred intervention (namely, jettisoning capitalism) is effective at improving the world. </span></p><hr /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Recently, many people on LessWrong have made structurally similar arguments about prosaic AI safety work. Here are </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/YiRsCfkJ2ERGpRpen/leogao-s-shortform#LehXdfD6KsbeNwZGk" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">two</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> such </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/4mtqQKvmHpQJ4dgj7/daniel-tan-s-shortform?commentId=Sbwpq8aBECvBSoFKC" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">shortforms</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> with &gt;100 karma presenting this argument, though it has been raised in many other places. It generally goes like this:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/uMQ3cqWDPHhjtiesc/agi-ruin-a-list-of-lethalities" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Aligning superintelligence is very difficult</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Prosaic AI safety measures, such as chain-of-thought monitoring for broken RL environments, address the symptoms of misalignment but don't solve the underlying problem, or at least don't scale to superintelligence. </span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Additionally, getting better at aligning current intelligences means that there are fewer containment breaches, and so there is less support for the best possible solution, an AI pause.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So actually, you should not do chain-of-thought monitoring. Ideally you should spend all of your time trying to Pause AI, but if you don't want to do that you should </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/6j3kBHdowGLCeqobg/dear-god-please-do-not-resign-in-protest?commentId=vrp2cBkobwfk2BcYW" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">do nothing</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefebvygp779e"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnebvygp779e">[4]</a></sup></span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The people who make these arguments make them in good faith. Nevertheless, I have both object-level and meta-level objections to their line of reasoning. My first object-level problem with these arguments is that I do think that prosaic AI safety efforts reduce the chance of runaway misaligned AI - even if they don't scale to superintelligence - and that the people making these arguments have confused theories of change. I've tried to articulate that disagreement in more detail </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PgJYwnN7fZKipgMz4/reevaluating-agi-ruin-a-list-of-lethalities-in-2026" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, but in no particular order: </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We are rapidly approaching AIs that are sufficient for bootstrapping recursive self-improvement. Current AIs inside companies like Anthropic already have a lot of say in making decisions to design their successors, so "mundane alignment of present-day AIs" is affecting the safety of future models.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Raising the ceiling for the most capable model we can use safely is good. This delays AI takeover, and therefore allows us to develop new prosaic safety techniques, or refine the ones we have. Techniques need not "scale to superintelligence" in order to be helpful in this way.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Arguments that current alignment techniques break down at some level of intelligence do not usually allow us to predict </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">when</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> they should break down, or at what level of capability. And if we can't tell </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">when</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> they break down, they might very well break down </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">after</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> AI models are then capable of making further prosaic contributions, or else make fundamental advances in e.g. mechanistic interpretability. </span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI progress is not currently bottlenecked on alignment. This seems like something that alignment people say copefully but is wrong. The two things that matter for AI progress are frontier labs' data efficiency and compute efficiency, two highly quantifiable metrics that can be improved even with models that don't care a whole lot about people or generalize well to other tasks. </span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Further, the entire paradigm of asking whether a particular technique "scales to superintelligence" seems wrong and confused to me, somewhat akin to asking if hashing passwords "scales to APTs". The question to ask is not whether a technique is </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">sufficient</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to contain superintelligence, but whether it could help guard against correlated failures in other components. Derisively call this a "grab bag" approach if you want, but that phrase can be equally used to describe many useful practices in fields like information security. </span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My meta-level objection is that "AI safety of superintelligence" is a pre-paradigmatic field for which there are no feedback loops, and indeed no superintelligence, and so sacrifices to the greater good are even more speculative than when they're made in service of political ideologies. That doesn't mean that every approach is equal, and it certainly doesn't mean that attention is correctly allocated. But it does mean we should be cautious about encouraging others to abandon approaches that work on current models, if the counterfactual is for them to do nothing. </span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn7xwx74xqbo9"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref7xwx74xqbo9">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I am lampooning a bit, but not by much. </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnj4zxm1qgqdt"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefj4zxm1qgqdt">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From "</span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Soul_of_Man_Under_Socialism" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Soul of Man Under Socialism</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">". When I started writing this post I was under the mistaken impression that this quote was from Frederick Douglass. In </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My Bondage and My Freedom </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and other places</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Douglass actually repeatedly claimed the opposite. I find it telling that the actual former slave who I thought wrote this quote believed that good treatment of slaves encouraged them to meditate on their own situation, and that the quote was instead written by a white socialist several decades after slavery had been abolished in the U.S. and Britain. </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnox69qtkq9w"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefox69qtkq9w">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As an unstated corollary, the evil capitalists, such as the guys involved in the BP Oil Spill disaster, are the True Heroes of the revolution, inasmuch as they do not then use their wealth to cement political conditions.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnebvygp779e"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefebvygp779e">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As an unstated corollary, Elon Musk is currently the most effective altruist, because </span><a href="https://goodhartlabs.com/honeybench" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">his model is very likely to breach containment in the near future</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and he is not currently reducing time-to-AGI except indirectly through competitive pressure, because Grok is not at the frontier.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/WRvxRHMCmc3Qt4axz/greater-good-arguments-in-ai-safety#comments">Discuss</a>

### [The Alignment Journal: Our Approach and Related Projects](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/xGucXHQ3NLkHZL3Cg/the-alignment-journal-our-approach-and-related-projects)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The </span></b><a href="https://alignmentjournal.org" rel="noreferrer"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alignment Journal</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is now open for submissions!</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is the fifth post in a series: </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/msnGbm52ZcG3xYcFo/an-alignment-journal-coming-soon"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">announcement post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> introduced the journal's core premise and its three key innovations: paid attributed review, published review summaries,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefwlzmn0yi6r"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnwlzmn0yi6r">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and LLM-assisted workflow;</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/hvq7amw8FKZxEeKqA/an-alignment-journal-features-and-policies"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">features and policies post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> examined the shortcomings of existing venues and argued for the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/hvq7amw8FKZxEeKqA/an-alignment-journal-features-and-policies#Journal_not_conference"></a><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/hvq7amw8FKZxEeKqA/an-alignment-journal-features-and-policies#Journal_not_conference"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">advantages of a journal over a conference</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">; </span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/hvq7amw8FKZxEeKqA/an-alignment-journal-features-and-policies#Journal_not_conference"></a><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/iNEpvPF8JeDKh8Dpe"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Adaptation to AI post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">described how we expect AI progress to shape the journal; and</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/hvq7amw8FKZxEeKqA/an-alignment-journal-features-and-policies#Journal_not_conference"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></a><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9vm2wtAtb34pEkjje/the-alignment-journal-organization-personnel-and-scope"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Organization, Personnel, and Scope post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">announced our editorial board, scope, and acceptance criteria. </span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In this post we go deeper: presenting our&nbsp;</span><a href="https://puppypaste.com/#Theory_of_Change"></a><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Theory_of_change" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">theory of change</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;— including the status and information </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Prestige_and_information_externalities_in_scholarly_publication" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">economics of the journal</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">; a survey of&nbsp;</span><a href="https://puppypaste.com/#Appendix_3__Prior_Art"></a><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix_1__Prior_Art" rel="noreferrer"></a><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix__Prior_Art"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">previous related projects</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">; and the high-level conclusions that follow from this analysis. We list some of the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Key_performance_indicators" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">key performance indicators</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> we'll track, the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Downside_risk_pre_mortem" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">downside scenarios</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> we must avoid, and possible </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Extensions" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">future extensions</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of the journal.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The journal is being built to serve the alignment research community. This post’s purpose is to solicit feedback and encourage you to sign up on </span></b><a href="https://www.alignmentjournal.org/" rel="noreferrer"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">alignmentjournal.org</span></b></a><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> if you want to participate.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;Our plans will be continually revised.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Goals</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The primary function of a journal is to bring together exceptional researchers and focus their collective attention on identifying and elevating the best work. To reprise, our goals in concert with this are:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Improve and disseminate research on alignment that emphasizes advances in scientific and conceptual understanding.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8wsvy0c53tn"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8wsvy0c53tn">[2]</a></sup></span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Combine the best features from traditional journals, ML conferences, internet forums, and social media while avoiding their pathologies: existing academic venues can be slow, opaque, myopic, and waste expert effort, while forums lack sufficient depth, filtering, and legible certification.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">More accurately match papers to the best reviewers based on expertise and interest, making review more rigorous and carefully rationing scarce expert attention.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Bring researchers from traditional academia, frontier labs, and independent organizations together by providing a rigorously vetted, high-standards venue incentivizing deep contributions.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Filter and make legible the top alignment research results to outsiders, including to researchers in adjacent fields and to funders.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The innovations in review process, incentive design, and adaptation to AI tools are in service of the core proposition: leveraging the attention of people to accelerate progress in alignment. To that end, we must learn from the strengths and weaknesses of traditional journals, ML conferences, internet forums, and social media, drawing lessons from </span><a href="https://puppypaste.com/#Appendix_3__Prior_Art"></a><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix_1__Prior_Art"></a><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix__Prior_Art" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">previous and ongoing efforts to improve academic review</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, as carried out by, among others,&nbsp;</span><a href="https://jmlr.org/tmlr/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TMLR</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">,&nbsp;</span><a href="https://unjournal.org/"></a><a href="https://unjournal.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Unjournal</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://distill.pub/about/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Distill</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">,&nbsp;and&nbsp;</span><a href="https://blog.neurips.cc/2025/09/30/reflections-on-the-2025-review-process-from-the-program-committee-chairs/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">NeurIPS</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Theory of change</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The publication pipeline for academics and for AI safety researchers is ripe for disruption; there are many roadblocks to effective research that can be addressed with a well-designed intervention.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Prestige and information externalities in scholarly publication</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Journals generally provide two goods: curation and public dissemination of research, and career capital for researchers. A third — belonging to and sustaining a community of peers — is important and explains a lot of why academics participate, but it complements the other two rather than competing with them, so we set it aside. The incentives governing the first two functions are potentially in conflict but can be brought into better alignment through improved institutional design.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From the perspective of the community of research&nbsp;</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">users</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, the output of research — ideas, results, methods — is non-rival and imperfectly excludable, and its social value is maximized by broad dissemination and reuse. Research consumers need research outputs that are vetted for quality, cheap to access, easy to copy, and widely distributed. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From the perspective of the researcher, the relevant goods look different. Publication in a journal is best understood as an excludable and congestible good.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflefoan0chk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlefoan0chk">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The journal provides ratification of one’s results, scholarly and public attention toward them, and consequent prestige and career capital. A new journal in a field that lacks one offers the prospect of generating these benefits for kinds of work that existing venues underweight. As long as the pool of researchers seeking to publish is small, these goods might be only weakly rivalrous. As the number of submissions increases, they become more strongly rivalrous. A new journal has only a limited store of attention, prestige, and career capital that it can distribute without diluting these goods by accepting work below the quality bar. Peer review and rejection are congestion control devices, intended to preserve the value of these goods.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There is therefore a presumptive tension between how journals can serve their two constituencies. Authors benefit from exclusivity which confers attention, career capital, and prestige. Readers benefit from establishing a baseline of validity but not further gating research beyond that. In the face of meager response by the dominant commercial journals to address this conflict, preprint servers have increasingly assumed a role as a demand-side solution, satisfying the distributional interests of research consumers.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefog7fu2cikq"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnog7fu2cikq">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> But while the marginal cost of adding an additional reader to a digital artifact is close to zero, the marginal social benefit depends substantially on the step that preprint servers omit: validation of the research.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Separate validation from acclaim</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A journal can ease this tension by separating validation from recognition. It needs to do three things. First, validate: provide the review step that preprint servers do not, and publish only work that has passed such validation. Second, distribute openly: make every published paper as cheap to access, link and cite as an arXiv preprint. Together these serve readers. They do little for authors, though. Being certified as sound earns some career capital indirectly, through the attention and impact that validation unlocks, but is unlikely to unlock the clout of acceptance in some flagship organ. The third step, then, is to supply the scarce good separately: choose a small number of the most notable of the papers already published and give them awards that bring prestige and attention.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overlay journals already take the first two steps. They sit on top of a preprint server, run peer review, and certify accepted papers without hosting copies of their own. Some go further and add extra certifications for standout work. We plan to do all three, with a set of prizes for remarkable work at the top. If done right, these could jointly deliver the weight that publication in a highly selective journal carries in other fields. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This separation also solves a review-side problem. At a selective venue, reviewers are asked not only whether research is sound and worthwhile, but whether it deserves one of a limited number of slots: a rivalrous good that they themselves compete for, at the same time or at other times. It is unsurprising that this does not always produce constructive reviews. It is also an unreliable way to allocate acclaim, since reviewers generally do not see the field against which any given paper is competing.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Our acceptance bar is a serious one, but it is not a ranking. A paper </span><a href="https://www.alignmentjournal.org/open/policies/scope-and-acceptance-criteria" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">must</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> be correct, must advance our principled understanding, and must be the most valuable thing for at least a few researchers to read now. Each paper is judged against the bar rather than against the other submissions, so acceptance is not rivalrous in the sense above.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref1wm7vrxnej3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn1wm7vrxnej3">[5]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We plan to let reviewers nominate a paper for one of a small, fixed set of awards and make the case for it. Those decisions are made by a separate committee, chosen to minimize conflicts of interest, that sees the whole field of candidates. Acceptance thus signals that a paper has cleared a demanding bar, while the scarcer prestige is concentrated in the awards.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Separating validation from acclaim is not a new idea. </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PLOS_One"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">PLOS ONE</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> has reviewed for soundness alone since 2006, leaving importance for readers to judge after publication. eLife </span><a href="https://elifesciences.org/inside-elife/741dbe4d/elife-s-new-model-open-for-submissions"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">dropped accept/reject decisions</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in 2023. It now publishes reviewed preprints with an assessment that rates significance and strength of evidence on a </span><a href="https://elifesciences.org/about/elife-assessments"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">fixed vocabulary</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, a cousin of our review summaries. Closest to our design, </span><a href="https://jmlr.org/tmlr/editorial-policies.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TMLR</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> accepts papers on correctness and audience interest and recognizes significance through separate certifications. Its Outstanding certification can be awarded a year or more after publication. Its experience also points to the main risk of this approach: despite offering its own certifications, TMLR has faced a </span><a href="https://ncfrey.substack.com/p/publishing-and-communicating-research"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">perceived “prestige gap”</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> relative to the major ML conferences. We are betting that a more demanding acceptance bar will let acceptance carry weight in its own right, but that remains to be shown.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Remunerate reviewer labor</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Peer review runs, mostly, on unpaid labor. In research communities with relatively homogeneous career objectives, review reciprocity (reviewing in exchange for being reviewed) can incentivize participation. This is common in ML conferences,</span><a href="https://iclr.cc/Conferences/2026/AuthorGuide" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> e.g., ICLR, where reviewing is a condition of submitting</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. In alignment, many of the people best placed to review sit in commercial companies or nonprofit organizations, and may be indifferent to or actively discouraged from publication. They have nothing to get back. Reciprocity and community spirit alone are unlikely to sustain the collective epistemic enterprise of an alignment journal. We will bridge that incentive gap by paying reviewers.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Paid review has the additional advantage that payment can more straightforwardly be tied to desirable outcomes, such as review quality and insight, to avoid minimal-effort reviews that merely satisfy reciprocity conditions.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Paying for work people might have done out of goodwill can backfire. Payment can displace the motives it was meant to supplement, a risk we discussed in our </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/hvq7amw8FKZxEeKqA/an-alignment-journal-features-and-policies#Reviewer_compensation"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">features and policies post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftsze9zm016k"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntsze9zm016k">[6]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> One well-known finding is that small payments can do worse than none at all (</span><a href="https://research.tilburguniversity.edu/en/publications/pay-enough-or-dont-pay-at-all-2/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gneezy &amp; Rustichini 2000</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). This observation informs our design:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Payments are substantial, currently $500‒$1,700, so they read as a professional fee rather than a tip; we are explicitly valuing the work of reviewing.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Money is not the only reward. Reviews are attributed, and the review summary is a public, credited piece of work. Payment is an experiment we can scale back or drop if it misfires.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The evidence so far is encouraging but thin.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefr1etkpq7ni"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnr1etkpq7ni">[7]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">  We will track review quality, invitation acceptance rates and who agrees to review, and survey reviewers on why they took part.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Desired outcomes</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">With these adjustments, we think that alignment research can develop a culture of peer review that it has, to date, often lacked. The requirement to pass peer review incentivizes serious engagement with existing literature, and anticipation of and engagement with objections before publication rather than after. Despite its faults, peer review can elevate the assessments of a few reviewers who deeply engage with the work rather than aggregating up many surface-level responses as is often the case on social media, especially for work that has yet to attract widespread attention. Peer review can also create new opportunities to connect work to neighboring fields. </span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Bringing peer review and a systematic, widely-indexed publication process to alignment research can furthermore make it more legible to prospective funders and policy makers outside of the alignment research community, who might balk at treating online forums or personal blogs as a source of research ground truth, and at relying on karma for peer review.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefcllcyk5ozrb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fncllcyk5ozrb">[8]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Of course, many formal approaches to publication have led to systemic problems, such as contributing to a “publish or perish” mentality in which prestigious publications are seen as an end in themselves, instead of a means to advance a collective epistemic project. While there is no decisive way to avoid this risk, we can mitigate it by keeping acceptance a standard rather than a quota, and then allocating acclaim based not only on editor taste but on the observed usefulness of (and response to) a piece of research. The model should be prizes awarded ex post, in regular review cycles after publication, once a paper’s usefulness and reception can be observed, rather than decisions made entirely at acceptance.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Objections</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We also acknowledge that online forums combined with a tightly-knit research community allow for a kind of rapid iteration and experimentation that peer review inevitably slows down. But the Alignment Journal would not seek to replace the forums and blog posts where ideas are often first worked out. Instead, it would provide a venue of record where the fully-developed versions of those ideas could be validated and distributed to the broader research community. We have prepared a </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Downside_risk_pre_mortem" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">downside risk pre-mortem</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on these and other issues.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Key performance indicators</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These are some of the indicators that we will use to estimate the success of the journal, both subjective and objective. We will use these to diagnose problems, not as targets to optimize.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Who submits and reviews</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Where authors and reviewers come from:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the mix by sector (academia, frontier companies, independent) and by home discipline. </span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do researchers submit their best research to us?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> “Best” is subjective of course, but conditional on whose taste you trust this is pretty measurable.</span></li></ul><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Review process</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (matching, rigor, speed)</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Time between submission and publication</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. We expect things to start slow as we work out the kinks in the process, but our long-term goal is 1-2 months.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Time to first review, time in discussion and time to decision</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, tracked separately.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefmmsszkgzh0f"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnmmsszkgzh0f">[9]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We want everything to go fast.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Review quality as rated by action editors</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, plus reviewers’ self-rated expertise as a check on matching.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Authors’ rating of how useful the reviews were,</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> including authors of rejected papers.</span></li><li value="5"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do people engage with, cite, and discuss the review summaries?</span></b></li></ul><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Legibility and prestige</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (outsiders, funders, career capital)</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Article-level citations</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, including the share from outside the alignment community. </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(Eventually) </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Impact factor and related traditional journal metrics.</span></b><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefxrcjaij105j"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnxrcjaij105j">[10]</a></sup></span></li></ul><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Downside risk pre-mortem</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How could this journal have negative impact?</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Low-quality research.</span></b><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We could lower the status of the field by publishing low-quality work in the “official” journal of alignment research.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Poor management.</span></b><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we end up with “market power” but run the journal poorly, we could bring the field into disrepute and make it difficult for a better journal to get started.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Wrong focus. </span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The boundaries and value of different types of alignment research are hotly disputed. We could overly reward or promote research that proves fruitless, misallocating researchers' effort and misleading outsiders on progress. Mitigation requires </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9vm2wtAtb34pEkjje/the-alignment-journal-organization-personnel-and-scope#Acceptance_criteria" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">carefully defined criteria</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to make transparent decisions.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Narrow editorial taste becoming the field's definition of “real” alignment research.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The editorial board is broad, but cannot span the whole field, and many calls are taste-based and made with limited information. Moreover, different sub-domains of alignment have differing appetites for a journal, some areas being already well-served by the flagship conferences, so we face selection bias.</span></li><li value="5"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Lack of sustainability/persistence.</span></b><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If we become the venue of record for important work and then lose funding or otherwise fold, our site and its contents could vanish, memory-holing the research we published. The more successful we are, the worse this would be.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefmkfourkmrts"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnmkfourkmrts">[11]</a></sup></span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref1nb8cy1dwmd"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn1nb8cy1dwmd">[12]</a></sup></span></li><li value="6"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Publishing tools used by capabilities research.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> If we successfully improve academic publishing mechanisms, we could accelerate dangerous research such as the gain of offense-dominant capabilities or publish and elevate alignment work with capability externalities.</span></li><li value="7"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Waste of time.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We might simply induce alignment researchers to divert some of their effort into journal activities without sufficient upside.</span></li><li value="8"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Goodharting.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> If awards/acclaim provided by the Alignment Journal come to have significant material benefits for recipients, we could end up incentivizing optimizing for acclaim as distinct from advancing the collective epistemic project of making powerful AI go well. </span></li><li value="9"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Reputation-washing.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Frontier companies advancing AI capabilities could use the reputation and prestige of a publication in the journal to obscure harmful practices that do not live up to the standards advertised in their published work.</span></li><li value="10"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Crowding out intrinsic motivation.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Paying reviewers could displace the goodwill that sustains peer review elsewhere. It could attract reviewers who are mainly there for the fee, or make unpaid reviewing in the wider field feel like a lesser obligation. We will watch review quality and the makeup of our reviewer pool, and change the payment scheme if we see either effect.</span></li><li value="11"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Funder dependence or capture.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The journal’s business model depends upon philanthropic funding, thereby providing a mechanism through which the interests of funders may be imposed upon the journal. We seek to preserve our independence by relying upon a well-regarded editorial board to make decisions independently of such interests, staking reputation upon it. We welcome suggestions for further safeguards.</span></li></ul><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Extensions</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If the journal is successful, it could become a good platform for several possible extensions:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Replication bounties.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Offer payments to third parties to do post-publication replication for certain papers that the editors judge to be notable/important and are somewhat doubted by the community.</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(For the time being, full replication of a paper is probably too costly, even with recent AI tools, to be done routinely, which is why we think it should not be part of the normal review process.)</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is especially attractive in the light of potential sharing of expertise or infrastructure with similar organizations, e.g., </span><a href="https://www.unjournal.org" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Unjournal</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li></ol></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Public forecasts</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (or even prediction markets) about which publications will later achieve some objective correlate of importance or veracity, like citation count, replication, or mention in model cards or legislation. Some combination of editors, reviewers, or readers could participate.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Preregistration and registered reports.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We could encourage authors to preregister their hypotheses and analyses, and ask reviewers to check the paper against the registration.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefaeyzfc6nyof"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnaeyzfc6nyof">[13]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> A strong version is the </span><a href="https://www.cos.io/initiatives/registered-reports"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Registered Reports</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> format, now offered by over 300 journals.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Retrospective review of existing published papers</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, either</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">With author participation (similar to traditional peer review)</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Without author participation (i.e., third-party commentary)</span></li></ol></li><li value="5"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An in-person conference.</span></b></li><li value="6"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Journal-to-conference track.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> In agreement with one or more conferences, we could “certify” some high-quality subset of papers we publish to get automatic inclusion in a track at the conferences.</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">See </span><a href="https://medium.com/@TmlrOrg/tmlr-joins-neurips-icml-iclr-journal-to-conference-track-937a898eab3d"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TMLR’s blog post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on doing this for their journal with the NeurIPS, ICML, and ICLR conferences.</span></li></ol></li><li value="7"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Conference-to-journal track.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Is there virtue to anointing select papers with a DOI and a seal of approval? Note that we already permit substantial expansion of conference papers.</span></li><li value="8"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Review articles and pedagogy</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, perhaps commissioned, i.e., author payments. These might feature stronger editorial input and perhaps interactive content.</span></li></ol><hr /><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Appendix: Prior Art</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This section contains our thoughts on a few particular existing projects related to reforming journals: </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Distill,</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Journal of Open Source Software</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Unjournal</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Transactions on Machine Learning Research</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. We hope it gives a sense of how we think about these things.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Distill</span></h2><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Distill</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> was a highly regarded, widely read, and unusual ML journal which focused on clear communication and pedagogy. It went on indefinite hiatus after less than five years of operations, and </span><a href="https://distill.pub/2021/distill-hiatus/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">their post-mortem</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is strongly recommended reading. In our assessment, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Distill</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> had two main value-adds which, by the editors’ own assessment, were extremely labor-intensive:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">intense (50+ hour) mentorship to authors; and</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">web-native interactive and dynamic visualizations.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We believe these are worthy goals, and we hope that </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Distill</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> or another journal finds a way to take up this mantle again in a sustainable way; indeed, LLMs potentially greatly reduce the burden of building dynamic content. However, the primary goal of the Alignment Journal is distinct from Distill’s pedagogic goals: </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">efficient and legible</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">assessment of research</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. We will focus instead on optimizing the use of reviewer time and effort.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If the Alignment Journal overall is successful, we could imagine branching out to more pedagogical work with stronger editorial input and interactive content (among many other possibilities; see </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Extensions"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Extensions</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), but that would only be done with caution.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Journal of Open Source Software</span></h2><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The </span><a href="https://joss.theoj.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Journal of Open Source Software</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (JOSS) is a diamond open-access journal for research software demonstrating a wide range of innovative editorial and review strategies which have informed the Alignment Journal's thinking.</span></p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Authors submit short papers (under 1,000 words, in Markdown) and the journal reviews both the paper and the underlying code. It launched in May 2016 and by 2023 was publishing roughly one paper per day, having just hit its 2,000th paper — all with no article processing charges. For the year 2019, while publishing about 350 papers, they put their </span><a href="https://blog.joss.theoj.org/2019/06/cost-models-for-running-an-online-open-journal"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">cash costs at around $800</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (about $21,000 counting donated services but excluding volunteer effort from editors and reviewers).</span><p></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">JOSS solves a different problem — giving credit for software — than we aim to solve in the Alignment Journal. Rather than the specific details, we take from them the lesson that it is possible to do open-access peer review at scale, with a fully open-source stack, which they have done for a decade.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Automation as philosophy</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">JOSS automates remorselessly for </span><a href="https://blog.joss.theoj.org/2019/06/cost-models-for-running-an-online-open-journal" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">cost</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://www.arfon.org/chatops-driven-publishing" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">editor/reviewer efficiency</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. In practice this means proof generation from Markdown, reference DOI checking via the Crossref API, repository analysis (license detection, language detection, lines-of-code count), Crossref XML generation, DOI minting, paper hosting, reminder emails for overdue reviews, and final publication are all handled by software. The only human steps are scope decisions, reviewer matching, reading the paper, and a final proofread before acceptance.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Chatops-driven publishing</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Each review takes place in its own GitHub issue, with a JOSS bot participating. Assigning reviewers, generating proofs, checking references, and accepting papers are all done by typing </span><code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">@editorialbot &lt;command&gt;</span></code><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in that thread. This operational pattern is called “</span><a href="https://www.arfon.org/chatops-driven-publishing"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">chatops-driven publishing</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">” by founding editor Arfon Smith. This design has several beneficial consequences:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">New editors learn by reading existing reviews.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The commands are self-documenting; you do not need a training manual, just a few completed threads to read.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The audit trail is complete by construction.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Every state transition — reviewer assigned, checklist item checked, proof generated, acceptance triggered — is a timestamped event.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Operational dashboards are easy.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> All state lives in structured, queryable form; thus, showing editor workload, time-in-review, and bottlenecks is just a database query over existing data.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The underlying technology (a Ruby framework called </span><a href="https://github.com/openjournals/buffy"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Buffy</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> deployed on GitHub Issues) does not transfer to the Alignment Journal’s needs, which require semi-confidential review, per-user access controls, and paid workflow rather than public volunteer review. But the principle seems to contain design patterns worth investigating: keep editorial tooling </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">inside</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the review interface rather than in a separate system, and design every workflow around minimizing the number of clicks, context switches, and decisions an editor has to make. </span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Open-source infrastructure</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">JOSS's stack is four loosely-coupled components: </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a </span><a href="https://github.com/openjournals/joss"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Rails web app</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for submission intake and dashboards; </span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">an editorial bot framework (</span><a href="https://github.com/openjournals/buffy"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Buffy</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">); </span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a Pandoc-based document production pipeline (</span><a href="https://github.com/openjournals/inara"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Inara</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) that produces PDF, HTML, JATS XML, Crossref XML, and CFF from Markdown source; and </span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a </span><a href="https://github.com/openjournals/joss-papers"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">GitHub repository</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for archival storage with CI workflows for compilation and DOI deposit. </span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Each component can be replaced independently. Initial build cost was roughly $50K (0.25 FTE for a year). Their blog posts on </span><a href="https://blog.joss.theoj.org/2019/06/cost-models-for-running-an-online-open-journal"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">cost models</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://blog.joss.theoj.org/2019/07/scaling"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">scaling</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and </span><a href="https://blog.joss.theoj.org/2023/05/JOSS-publishes-2000th-paper"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">operations at 2,000 papers</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> are worth reading for anyone designing a journal's technical infrastructure.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Where JOSS has struggled</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Challenges that JOSS has faced are also worth noting for the Alignment Journal:</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Review time creeps upward.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Average time spent in review went from </span><a href="https://blog.joss.theoj.org/2023/05/JOSS-publishes-2000th-paper" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">under three months (pre-COVID) to about four months by 2023</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. JOSS remains uncertain why; likely contributors include reviewer fatigue as the pool gets overused, submission growth without proportional reviewer growth, and the general post-COVID decline in volunteer engagement. One lesson from this is to instrument review timelines from day one, tracking time-to-first-review, time-in-discussion, and time-to-decision separately. Once a slowdown becomes visible it is much easier to diagnose where the bottleneck actually is.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Scope disputes consume disproportionate editorial time.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> JOSS initially handled scope questions ad hoc on a mailing list, which consumed substantial editor effort. They eventually moved to automated flagging (submissions under 1,000 lines of code get flagged, under 300 get desk-rejected), a mandatory “Statement of Need” section, and a weekly batch review of flagged submissions by the editorial team. For the Alignment Journal, scope decisions seem likely to be at least as contested. A documented, regular, low-overhead process for scope decisions from the start (rather than handling each case ad hoc) seems substantially preferable.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Unjournal</span></h2><p><a href="https://unjournal.org/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Unjournal</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;offers an early-stage working example of an effort to unbundle expert evaluation from traditional journal publication. It is not itself a journal, and there are no accept/reject decisions. Instead, it overlays public evaluations (aka reviews), quantitative ratings, author responses, and manager syntheses on top of preprints and working papers, while remaining non-exclusive.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefpw4tvwo83ei"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnpw4tvwo83ei">[14]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> They focus on research with “potential for global impact” in economics, social science, policy, and forecasting rather than technical AI or ML.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefv6sgzqovrw"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnv6sgzqovrw">[15]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">&nbsp;Some aspects of their process are relevant here.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Substantive Innovations</span></h3><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Public evaluations/reviews/ratings</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">,&nbsp;</span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">editorial synthesis</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Unjournal publishes the full “evaluation packages” with DOIs for every research object they consider. This creates a rich public artifact for later readers, funders, and policy makers, and it makes the labor of review legible instead of discarding it after an editorial decision. We're unlikely to fully go down this route: our journal still uses optionally-confidential referee reports and editorial selection. However, our proposed review summaries are motivated by a similar goal: reviewer attention should produce something more informative than a one-bit accept/reject, and a summary of those findings should be prepared with the reader in mind. Their “</span><a href="https://unjournal.pubpub.org/prize-winning-evaluations"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">prize-winning evaluations</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">” provide some strong examples of value add by creating a richer signal.</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Structured evaluation and quantified ratings&nbsp;</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As The Unjournal does not use an accept/reject filter, they emphasize quantitative ratings and predictions as a measure of research prestige and credibility. We're unlikely to ask for this level of structured rating for a typical publication, although we plan to give a small number of awards.</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Prioritizing impact, links to impact-focused practitioners and stakeholders</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Unjournal's field specialist teams explicitly prioritize research for its potential real-world impact (along the lines discussed </span><a href="https://globalimpact.gitbook.io/the-unjournal-project-and-communication-space/policies-projects-evaluation-workflow/considering-projects/process-prioritizing-research/prioritization-ratings-discussion" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).
